Canonical transformations generated by the boundary volume: Unimodular and non-Abelian teleparallel gravity
Florian Girelli, Abdulmajid Osumanu, Wolfgang Wieland

Abstract
Recently, a new choice of variables was identified to understand how the quantum group structure appeared in three-dimensional gravity Dupuis:2020ndx . These variables are introduced via a canonical transformation generated by a boundary term. We show that this boundary term can actually be taken to be the volume of the boundary and that the new variables can be defined in any dimension greater than three. In addition, we study the associated metric and teleparallel formalisms. The former is a variant of the Henneaux–Teitelboim model for unimodular gravity. The latter provides a non-Abelian generalization of the usual Abelian teleparallel formulation.



I
Introduction

Quantum states are built from the representations of the symmetries, identified at the classical level. The structure of the symmetries depends on the choice of variables used to describe the phase space of the classical theory. In the gravity case, moving from the metric to the frame field variables (Palatini formalism) enlarges the set of gauge symmetries since internal Lorentz rotations are absent in the usual metric formalism. On the other hand, treating gravity as a constrained topological field theory
Freidel:1999rr
;
Freidel:2015gpa
has the opposite effect: the topological theory has a larger symmetry algebra. Gravity is recovered by imposing constraints that break the gauge symmetries down to those that appear in general relativity. Using the teleparallel formulation of gravity leads to yet another different type of symmetries, representing different representations at the quantum level (possibly related by dualities). Since both the metric and teleparallel formulations can be seen as a second-order formulation of gravity, it is interesting to study the relationship between the respective symmetries depending on the choice of variables. We refer to
Freidel:2020xyx
;
Freidel:2020svx
;
Freidel:2020ayo
;
DePaoli:2018erh
;
Oliveri:2019gvm
;
Wieland:2020gno
;
Wieland:2017zkf
;
Wieland:2017cmf
;
Wieland:2018ymr
for a recent discussion about these different issues in gravity.

In field theory, canonical transformations are typically generated by adding topological invariants or boundary terms to the action. Let us list some important examples in the context of gravity below (a more detailed discussion can be found in e.g.
Freidel:2020xyx
;
Freidel:2020svx
;
Freidel:2020ayo
;
DePaoli:2018erh
;
Oliveri:2019gvm
;
Wieland:2020gno
;
Wieland:2017zkf
;
Wieland:2017cmf
;
Wieland:2018ymr
and references therein).

•

In any dimension, the Gibbons-Hawking-York (boundary) term generates the canonical transformation changing the polarization from the superspace momentum representation, where the wave-functionals depend on the ADM momentum
\tilde{\pi}^{ab}=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\sqrt{\det{h}}\,(K^{ab}-h^{ab}K)
, to the position representation, where the wave-functional
\Psi[h_{ab}]
depends only on the intrinsic metric of the boundary, c.f.
adm
;
kiefer
;
thiemann
.

•

For gravity in four spacetime dimensions, the Holst term induces the Ashtekar–Barbero canonical transformation from the ADM-type of variables to the (complex) Ashtekar variables, which greatly simplifies the structure of the constraints, enabling the loop quantization of gravity
newvariables
;
Barbero1994
;
holst-action
;
thiemann
.

•

In three-dimensions, quantum groups play an important role in both the quantization of Chern–Simons theory
Fock:1998nu
and the discrete path integral formulation
TV
of gravity. Quantum groups appear as deformed gauge symmetries, where the quantum deformation is parametrized by the cosmological constant. This is in striking contrast to the classical formulation of three-dimensional gravity in the first-order formalism, where the gauge symmetries do
not
depend on the cosmological constant. It was recently found that a canonical transformation, induced by a boundary term, could be used to have a (bulk) theory defined in terms of new variables, such that the gauge symmetries do depend
now
on the cosmological constant as well, see
Dupuis:2020ndx
. This provided a direct manner to retrieve the quantum group symmetries upon quantization.

It has been conjectured that quantum groups could also be relevant for quantum gravity in four dimensions
Smolin:2002sz
;
Fairbairn:2010cp
;
Han:2010pz
, so one might wonder whether the canonical transformation or the boundary term that was identified in
Dupuis:2020ndx
can also be introduced in four dimensions. We show here that this is indeed the case and that there is a straightforward generalization of the boundary term in the first-order formalism. It is then natural to ask what happens to the second-order formalism and how the metric and teleparallel formulations of gravity could be expressed in terms of the new variables. Here, we answer these questions. In the metric formalism, we have a new notion of the Christoffel symbol. The volume term that is added to the action via the cosmological constant is replaced by the divergence of a vector field, whose norm depends on the cosmological constant. In fact, we recover a variant of the Henneaux–Teitelboim model for unimodular gravity
Henneaux:1989zc
. On the other hand, in the teleparallel formalism
tele
, the new variables suggest a deformed notion of torsion, which defines a new and non-Abelian version of the standard teleparallel formulation.

The boundary term that generates the canonical transformation that was introduced in
Dupuis:2020ndx
depends on an internal vector
r^{I}
, whose norm sets the value of the cosmological constant. In order to not increase the number of degrees of freedom, it was assumed that the vector is an auxiliary background structure, whose field-variations vanish on the covariant phase space, i.e.
\delta[r^{I}]=0
. However, the boundary term
Dupuis:2020ndx
has a very strong resemblance with the volume term of the boundary. It seems natural, therefore, to relax the condition
\delta[r^{I}]=0
. In fact, we will show that if we actually interpret the vector
r^{I}
as the
internal normal

n^{I}
to the boundary, the generating functional
Dupuis:2020ndx
is exactly the volume of the boundary. In here, we show that given this interpretation, we can allow for
\delta[n^{I}]\neq 0
and
still
perform the canonical transformation,
without
adding any new degrees of freedom in the bulk.

The new formulation has some interesting relations with already existing frameworks.

•

The boundary term plays an important role in holographic renormalization
Bianchi:2001kw
;
Skenderis:2002wp
so that our new variables will also be relevant to this framework.

•

With the change of variables, the volume term that scales with the cosmological constant drops out of the action and is replaced by the divergence of a vector field and a constraint which sets the cosmological constant as the norm of this vector field. We will see below that the resulting action defines a variation of the Henneaux–Teitelboim model for unimodular gravity
Henneaux:1989zc
.

One can often see the first-order formalism for gravity as a gauge theory, so that the canonical variables are Lie algebra-valued differential forms. This is obviously realized in three dimension in the Chern–Simons formulation. In four dimensions, the McDowell–Mansouri formulation
MacDowell:1977jt
provides a similar realization. Depending on the value of the cosmological constant and the signature of the metric, one usually deals with a (anti-)de Sitter or Poincaré (or Euclidian) gauge group, where the frame field has values in the boost or translation sector of the group. Accordingly, the (anti-)de Sitter or Poincaré curvature splits into the Lorentzian curvature sector, which depends on the cosmological constant, and the translational or boost sector, which represents torsion and is independent of
\Lambda
. Since the (anti-)de Sitter or Poincaré groups can be seen as the semi-cross product between the Lorentz transformations and the boosts or translations, there is a natural action of the Lorentz group on the frame field. On the other hand, there is no action of translations back onto rotations (the commutator of a translation and a rotation has no translational part).

The canonical transformation that we will study in here amounts to choosing a different decomposition of the (anti-)de Sitter group (the Poincaré case can also be treated in the same way). Indeed, if the standard formulation amounts to use the Cartan decomposition, then the canonical transformation instead picks the Iwasawa decomposition. We have then a double cross product relation and a more symmetric treatment between the connection and the frame field: the connection acts on the frame field, and the frame field acts back on the connection. As a consequence the (anti-)de Sitter or Poincaré curvature can be split differently than before. We get a generalized notion of torsion, which can be viewed as a (non-Abelian) curvature in the frame field sector. This is especially relevant regarding the teleparallel formulation.

We finally note that the boundary term could also be added in the usual metric formulation of General Relativity. We expect that this new boundary term will induce a canonical transformation from pure metric gravity to a version of unimodular gravity, similar to the one introduced in this paper. Since we are primarily interested in approaches to quantum gravity based on the first-order formalism, we do not explore this thread in this paper.

Outline
:

The article is organized as follows. In
section
II
, we review the gauge theory framework when dealing with a matched pair (double cross product) of Lie algebras, which will conveniently set up the conventions for the rest of the paper. In
section
III
, we discuss how the canonical transformation can be implemented in any dimension by the boundary term given in terms of the volume of the boundary. In
section
IV.1
, we determine the metric formulation in the new variables, while in
section
IV.2
, we determine the dual teleparallel formulation.

II
Gauge theory for a matched pair of Lie groups

In the following, we develop the notion of a gauge theory for a matched pair of Lie algebras. This structure generalizes the more common semi-direct product that can be found in the Poincaré case or in the decomposition of the Lorentz group in terms of rotations and boosts (although they do not form a Lie algebra). Matched pairs are the relevant structure which gives rise to the quantum group structure at the quantum level. For example, in the three-dimensional gravity case. A reader who would be more geometry inclined can skip this section, which nevertheless emphasizes that what is called torsion can also be viewed as a type of curvature.

The algebraic background behind the matched pairs can be found in
Majid:1996kd
. We call a matched pair
{\mathfrak{G}}={\mathfrak{g}}\bowtie{\mathfrak{h}}
of Lie algebras the pair of Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
, with respective Lie brackets
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{g}}
and
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}}
, equipped with two bilinear maps

\lhd:{\mathfrak{h}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{h}},(x,\alpha)\mapsto x\lhd\alpha
and
\rhd:{\mathfrak{h}}\times{\mathfrak{g}}\rightarrow{\mathfrak{g}},(x,\alpha)\mapsto x\rhd\alpha
, such that
{\mathfrak{h}}
is a right
{\mathfrak{g}}
-module and
{\mathfrak{g}}
is a left
{\mathfrak{h}}
-module. More intuitively,
{\mathfrak{g}}
acts from the right on
{\mathfrak{h}}
via
\lhd
and there is a back action
\rhd
of
{\mathfrak{h}}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
. To guarantee the Jacobian identity on
{\mathfrak{G}}
, the left and right actions must satisfy the following compatibility conditions:
\forall\alpha,\beta\in{\mathfrak{g}},\,x,y\in{\mathfrak{h}}
,

\displaystyle x\rhd[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=[x\rhd\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}+[\alpha,x\rhd\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}+(x\lhd\alpha)\rhd\beta-(x\lhd\beta)\rhd\alpha,

(1a)

\displaystyle[x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}}\lhd\alpha

\displaystyle=[x\lhd\alpha,y]_{\mathfrak{h}}+\,[x,y\lhd\alpha]_{\mathfrak{h}}+\,x\lhd(y\rhd\alpha)-y\lhd(x\rhd\alpha).

(1b)

As a vector space,
{\mathfrak{g}}\bowtie{\mathfrak{h}}
is isomorphic to
{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}
. If we introduce a matched basis, such that
(\alpha,x)\in{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}\sim{\mathfrak{g}}\bowtie{\mathfrak{h}}
, we may express the Lie bracket of
{\mathfrak{G}}
in terms of
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{g}}
,
[\cdot,\cdot]_{\mathfrak{h}}
,
\lhd
and
\rhd
,

\big[(\alpha,x),(\beta,y)\big]\equiv\big([\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}+x\rhd\beta-y\rhd\alpha,\,[x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}}+x\lhd\beta-y\lhd\alpha\big),\quad\forall\alpha,\beta\in{\mathfrak{g}},\quad x,y\in{\mathfrak{h}}.

(2)

In particular, there is the mixed bracket

\big[(0,x),(\beta,0)\big]\equiv[x,\beta]=x\lhd\beta+x\rhd\beta\in{\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}}.

(3)

Notice that the left and right modules can be written in terms of the Lie bracket alone,

x\lhd\beta:=[x,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},\quad x\rhd\beta:=[x,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}},

(4)

where
[x,\beta]|_{{V}}
is the projection of the Lie bracket
[\cdot,\cdot]
on the vector space
V
. With this notation, the compatibility relations (
1
) become

\displaystyle x\rhd[\alpha,\beta]_{\mathfrak{g}}

\displaystyle=\big[[x,\alpha]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}},\beta\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}+\big[\alpha,[x,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\big]_{\mathfrak{g}}+\big[[x,\alpha]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},\beta\big]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}-\big[[x,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},\alpha\big]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}},

(5a)

\displaystyle[x,y]_{\mathfrak{h}}\lhd\alpha

\displaystyle=\big[[x,\alpha]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},y\big]_{\mathfrak{h}}+\big[x,[y,\alpha]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}\big]_{\mathfrak{h}}+\big[x,[y,\alpha]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\big]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}-\big[y,[x,\alpha]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\big]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}.

(5b)

Consider then a gauge connection
\boldsymbol{A}
for such a Lie algebra, i.e. a
{\mathfrak{G}}
-valued one-form on a manifold
\mathcal{M}
, and consider its different components with respect to
the matched Lie algebra
{\mathfrak{g}}\bowtie{\mathfrak{h}}
. This type of gauge theory was studied by Majid
Majid:1996kd
at the discrete level of parallel propagators (holonomies). Below we will derive the infinitesimal picture.

Let us denote by
\gamma
and
h
the components
1
1

1

We will relate
(\gamma,h)
to the spin connection
\omega
and the frame field
e
.
of such a gauge connection with respect to the Lie algebras
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
.
The
{\mathfrak{g}}\bowtie{\mathfrak{h}}
Lie algebra-valued gauge connection takes the form

\boldsymbol{A}=\gamma+h\in({\mathfrak{g}}\oplus{\mathfrak{h}})\otimes\Lambda^{1}(M).

(6)

Due to the kick-back action between
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
, there is a non-trivial mixing between the components of the
{\mathfrak{g}}\bowtie{\mathfrak{h}}
basis, see (
4
). Consider, for example, a
{\mathfrak{G}}
-valued
p
-form
\boldsymbol{B}=(\beta,b)
. Its exterior covariant derivative is

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{B}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\beta+\mathrm{d}b+[\gamma+h,\beta+b]=\mathrm{d}\beta+\mathrm{d}b+[\gamma,\beta]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[\gamma,b]+[h,b]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}+[h,\beta]

(7)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}\beta+[\gamma,\beta]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[\gamma,b]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}+[h,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\right)+\left(\mathrm{d}b+[h,b]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}+[\gamma,b]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}+[h,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}\right).

For the individual component fields
(\beta,0)
and
(0,b)
, we have the covariant derivatives

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{|{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\beta

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\beta+[\gamma,\beta]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[h,\beta]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}},

(8)

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{|{{\mathfrak{h}}}}b

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}b+[\gamma,b]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}+[h,b]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}.

(9)

Due to the kick-back actions between the two Lie algebras, the covariant derivatives depend on both connection components
\gamma
and
h
.
Consider then the components of the field strength
\boldsymbol{F}
in the directions of
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}[\boldsymbol{A}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{A}+{\frac{1}{2}}[\boldsymbol{A}\wedge\boldsymbol{A}]=\mathrm{d}\gamma+\mathrm{d}h+{\frac{1}{2}}\big[(\gamma+h)\wedge(\gamma+h)\big]

(10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\gamma+\mathrm{d}h+{\frac{1}{2}}[\gamma\wedge\gamma]_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}}+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}}+{\frac{1}{2}}[h\wedge h]_{{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}\gamma+{\frac{1}{2}}[\gamma\wedge\gamma]_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}}\right)+\left(\mathrm{d}h+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}}+{\frac{1}{2}}[h\wedge h]_{{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}}\right)\equiv{\cal F}+{\cal T}.

The physical significance of splitting
\boldsymbol{F}
into
{\cal F}\in{\mathfrak{g}}\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\mathcal{M})
and
{\cal T}\in{\mathfrak{h}}\otimes\Lambda^{2}(\mathcal{M})
will be clear below. We will identify, in fact,
{\cal F}
and
{\cal T}
with a deformed version of
{\mathfrak{h}}
-valued torsion and
{\mathfrak{g}}
-valued curvature,

\displaystyle{\cal F}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}\gamma+{\frac{1}{2}}[\gamma\wedge\gamma]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\equiv F+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{\mathfrak{g}}},

(11)

\displaystyle{\cal T}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}h+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}+{\frac{1}{2}}[h\wedge h]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\equiv T+{\frac{1}{2}}[h\wedge h]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}.

(12)

Again, the curvature components depend on both connection types. The Bianchi identity for each component is

\displaystyle 0=\mathrm{d}_{\boldsymbol{A}}\boldsymbol{F}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}{\cal F}+\mathrm{d}{\cal T}+\big[(\gamma+h)\wedge({\cal F}+{\cal T})\big]

(13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}{\cal F}+[\gamma\wedge{\cal F}]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}\,+[h\wedge{\cal F}]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}+[\gamma\wedge{\cal T}]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\right)+\left(\mathrm{d}{\cal T}+[\gamma\wedge{\cal T}]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,+[h\wedge{\cal F}]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}+[\gamma\wedge{\cal T}]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}\right).

Let us consider then the gauge transformations of such a
{\mathfrak{G}}
-valued connection. Let
\boldsymbol{\lambda}=(\phi,t)
be a
{\mathfrak{G}}
-valued scalar, which generates an infinitesimal transformation of the connection
\boldsymbol{A}=(\gamma,h)
,

\displaystyle\delta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}[\boldsymbol{A}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}\boldsymbol{\lambda}+[\boldsymbol{A},\boldsymbol{\lambda}]=\left(\mathrm{d}\phi+[\gamma,\phi]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[h,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}+[\gamma,t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\right)+\left(\mathrm{d}t+[h,t]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}+[\gamma,t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}+[h,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}\right).

(14)

For pure rotations (
t=0
) and pure translations (
\phi=0
), we find

\left.\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\delta_{\phi}[\gamma]&=\mathrm{d}\phi+[\gamma,\phi]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[h,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}=\boldsymbol{D}_{|{\mathfrak{g}}}\phi,&\delta_{\phi}[h]&=[h,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},\\
\delta_{t}[\gamma]&=[\gamma,t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}},&\delta_{t}[h]&=\mathrm{d}t+[h,t]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}+[\gamma,t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}=\boldsymbol{D}_{|{\mathfrak{h}}}t.\end{array}\hskip 5.0pt\right\}

(15)

What is nice about these transformations is that the variation of
\gamma
or
h
in each of the different components is either a commutator or a derivative. In other words, the two components of the curvature two-form behave completely analogous under gauge transformations. Since
\delta_{\boldsymbol{\lambda}}[\boldsymbol{F}]=[\boldsymbol{F},\boldsymbol{\lambda}]
, we obtain

\displaystyle[{\cal F}+{\cal T},\phi+t]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[{\cal F},\phi]_{\mathfrak{g}}+[{\cal F},t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}+[{\cal T},\phi]\big|_{{\mathfrak{g}}}+[{\cal T},t]_{\mathfrak{h}}+[{\cal F},t]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}+[{\cal T},\phi]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\,.

(16)

In other words,

\left.\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\delta_{\phi}[{\cal F}]&=[{\cal F},\phi]_{\mathfrak{g}}+[{\cal T},\phi]\big|_{{\mathfrak{g}}},&\delta_{\phi}[{\cal T}]&=[{\cal T},\phi]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\\
\delta_{t}[{\cal F}]&=[{\cal F},t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}},&\delta_{t}[{\cal T}]&=[{\cal T},t]_{\mathfrak{h}}+[{\cal F},t]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}.\end{array}\hskip 5.0pt\right\}

(17)

A simple, but non trivial, example of such a matched pair of Lie algebras is the Poincaré Lie algebra. It is a semi-cross product
{\mathfrak{G}}={\mathfrak{g}}{\rhd\!\!\!<}{\mathfrak{h}}
, where
{\mathfrak{g}}
is either of the Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(n)
or
\mathfrak{so}(n-1,1)
, depending on the signature of spacetime, and
{\mathfrak{h}}\sim\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the Abelian Lie algebra of translations on
\mathbb{R}^{n}
. The respective generators are the generators of (Lorentz) rotations
J_{MN}=-J_{NM}
and translations
P_{M}
, which are labelled by spacetime indices
M,N,\dots=1,\dots,n
.

In the Poincaré context, there is no kick-back action of
{\mathfrak{h}}
on
{\mathfrak{g}}
(recall
[J,P]\propto P
). Given (
4
), we thus have

(\phi,t)\in{\mathfrak{g}}{\rhd\!\!\!<}{\mathfrak{h}},\quad t\lhd\phi:=[t,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},\quad t\rhd\phi:=[t,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}=0.

(18)

The translations commute, i.e.
[\cdot,\cdot]_{{\mathfrak{h}}}=0
, and the covariant derivative for the component fields
(\phi,0)
and
(0,t)
with values in
{\mathfrak{g}}
or
{\mathfrak{h}}
is given by

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{D}_{|{\mathfrak{g}}}\phi=\mathrm{d}\phi+[\gamma,\phi]_{{\mathfrak{g}}},\quad\boldsymbol{D}_{|{\mathfrak{h}}}t=\mathrm{d}t+[\gamma,t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}.

(19)

Consider then the components of the curvature two-form
\boldsymbol{F}
in the rotational and translational directions
{\mathfrak{g}}
and
{\mathfrak{h}}
of the Poincaré Lie algebra,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{F}[\boldsymbol{A}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\left(\mathrm{d}\gamma+{\frac{1}{2}}[\gamma\wedge\gamma]_{{{\mathfrak{g}}}}\right)+\left(\mathrm{d}h+[\gamma\wedge h]\big|_{{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}}\right)=F+T.

(20)

We recognize the usual notion of curvature
F
and torsion
T
, provided we identify
\gamma
with the spin connection
\omega
and
h
with the frame field
e
.

The infinitesimal gauge transformations for pure rotations (
t=0
) and pure translations (
\phi=0
) are

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\delta_{\phi}[\gamma]&=\mathrm{d}\phi+[\gamma,\phi]_{{\mathfrak{g}}}=\boldsymbol{D}_{|{\mathfrak{g}}}\phi,&\quad\delta_{\phi}[h]&=[h,\phi]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}},\\
\delta_{t}[\gamma]&=0,&\delta_{t}[h]&=\mathrm{d}t+[\gamma,t]\big|_{{{\mathfrak{h}}}}=\boldsymbol{D}_{|{\mathfrak{h}}}t,\end{array}

(21)

where
\delta_{\phi}[\gamma]
is the
\mathfrak{so}
gauge transformation for the spin connection
\omega=\gamma
, and
\delta_{t}[h]
is an infinitesimal translation of the frame field
h=e
. Finally, there are the Poincaré transformations of the curvature two-form

\begin{array}[]{rlrl}\delta_{\phi}[F]&=[F,\phi]_{\mathfrak{g}},&\qquad\delta_{\phi}[T]&=[T,\phi]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}\\
\delta_{t}[F]&=0,&\qquad\delta_{t}[T]&=[F,t]\big|_{{\mathfrak{h}}}.\end{array}

(22)

Although the translations
t\in{\mathfrak{h}}
are Abelian, a general such translation will act non-trivially on the translational curvature (torsion). If we would choose, however, a connection
\gamma
such that
F[\gamma]=0
, both the torsion component
T
and the rotational (Lorentz) component
F
of the Poincaré connection would be translational invariant. This choice underlies the teleparallel equivalent of general relativity.

In the following, we will consider the more complicated case of
{\mathfrak{G}}=\mathfrak{so}(n-1,1)\sim\mathfrak{so}(n-1)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{n-1}
, which is an example of a matched Lie algebra induced by the Iwasawa decomposition of the Lorentz Lie algebra
\mathfrak{so}(n,1)
.

III
Canonical transformation induced by the boundary volume term

III.1
Warm up: three-dimensions

Consider the three-dimensional Einstein – Cartan action in first-order spin-connection variables with a cosmological constant
\Lambda
, together with the following boundary terms, whose significance will become clear below,

\displaystyle S_{{\text{EC}}}[e,A|n]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\bigg[\int_{\mathcal{M}}{\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{K}\wedge\Big(R^{IJ}[A]-\frac{\Lambda}{3}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\Big)}

\displaystyle\hskip 70.0001pt-2\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\Big(s\,\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{K}\wedge n^{I}\mathrm{d}_{A}n^{J}+\frac{\sqrt{|\Lambda|}}{2}\varepsilon_{IJK}n^{K}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\Big)\bigg].

(23)

The action in the bulk is a functional of the Lorentz curvature
\mathchoice{R^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{R^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{R^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{R^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}[A]=\mathrm{d}\mathchoice{A^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{A^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884 and the triad
e^{I}
. The boundary term consists of two parts. The first term is the usual Gibbons – Hawking – York boundary term for first-order spin connection variables, where
\mathrm{d}_{A}[\cdot]=\mathrm{d}[\cdot]+[A,\cdot]
is the covariant exterior derivative. The internal vector field
n^{I}
is constrained to lie orthogonal to the boundary, i.e.
n_{I}\varphi_{\partial\mathcal{M}}^{\ast}e^{I}=0
, where
\varphi_{\partial\mathcal{M}}^{\ast}:T^{\ast}\mathcal{M}\rightarrow T^{\ast}{\partial\mathcal{M}}
is the pull-back. In addition,
n^{I}
is normalized such that
s=\eta_{IJ}n^{I}n^{J}=n_{I}n^{I}=\{\pm 1,0\}
, depending on whether the boundary is spacelike, timelike, or null.
2
2

2

In the Lorentzian case, our metric signature is
(-
+
+
\dots)
. Internal spacetime indices
I,J,K,\dots
are raised and lowered with the internal metric tensors
\eta^{IJ}
and
\eta_{IJ}
. Total antismmetrization of indices
I_{1},I_{2},\dots
is obtained via
\omega_{[I_{1}\dots I_{n}]}=\frac{1}{n!}\sum_{\sigma\in S_{n}}(-1)^{\sigma}\omega_{I_{\sigma(1)}\dots I_{\sigma(n)}}
.
Its orientation is such that
n^{a}:=n^{I}\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
is the outwardly oriented normal to the boundary. If the torsionless equation is satisfied, then the first term is the integral of the trace of the extrinsic curvature. The second term is proportional to the induced volume of the boundary.
The boundary conditions are

\mathchoice{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}\varphi^{\ast}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\delta[e^{J}]=0,\qquad\varphi^{\ast}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}\delta[n_{I}e^{I}]=0,

(24)

where
\delta[\cdot]
is a variation on the infinite-dimensional space of kinematical histories, and
h_{IJ}=-sn_{I}n_{J}+\eta_{IJ}
is the internal projector onto the boundary.

It is also useful to evaluate the action (
23
) for a spin connection
\mathchoice{A^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{A^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{A^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{A^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}
, which is torsionless (by going
half-shell
). We obtain

S_{\text{EC}}[A,e|n]\Big|_{\mathrm{d}_{A}e=0}=S_{\text{EH}}[g|n]=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\left[\int_{\mathcal{M}}d^{3}v\big(R[g]-2\Lambda\big)+2\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}d^{2}v\big(sK-\sqrt{|\Lambda|}\big)\right],

(25)

where
R[g]
is the Ricci scalar for the metric
g_{ab}=e_{Ia}\mathchoice{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[3.51666pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.1029pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.50208pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{b}}}}
, and
K=\nabla_{a}n^{a}
is the trace of the extrinsic curvature. In addition,
d^{3}v
and
d^{2}v
are the canonical volume elements on
\mathcal{M}
and
\partial{\mathcal{M}}
.
3
3

3

The volume elements are
p
-forms
d^{3}v=\frac{1}{6}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\wedge e^{K}
and
d^{2}v=\frac{1}{2}n^{I}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{J}\wedge e^{K}
.
The volume term appears in the definition of the bulk plus boundary action (
25
) to cancel infrared divergencies that would otherwise appear when the boundary
\partial\mathcal{M}
is sent to infinity
Bianchi:2001kw
.

Consider then a region
\Sigma\subset\partial\mathcal{M}
within the boundary. The first variation of the action for given boundary conditions (
24
) determines the pre-symplectic potential
\Theta_{\Sigma}^{\text{EC}}
on the space of physical histories, i.e. the space of solutions to the field equations. A straightforward calculation gives

\Theta^{\text{EC}}_{\Sigma}(\delta)=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\Sigma}\Big(s\,\varepsilon_{IJ}\delta[e^{I}]\wedge K^{J}-\sqrt{|\Lambda|}\,\varepsilon_{IJ}e^{I}\wedge\delta[e^{J}]\Big)-\frac{1}{8\pi G}\oint_{\partial\Sigma}s\,\varepsilon_{IJ}e^{I}\delta[n^{J}],

(26)

where
K^{I}=\varphi^{\ast}_{\partial M}\mathrm{d}_{A}n^{I}
is the extrinsic curvature (a one-form along the boundary), and
\varepsilon_{IJ}=n^{K}\varepsilon_{KIJ}
is the internal area element at the boundary. Notice that the variation
\delta[n^{I}]
of the internal normal only affects a corner term to the pre-symplectic potential (
26
). This is a consequence of the torsionless condition
\mathrm{d}_{A}e^{I}=0
and the boundary constraints
n_{I}n^{I}=s
and
\varphi^{\ast}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}(n_{I}e^{I})=0
, hence
n_{I}\delta[n^{I}]=0
and
\varphi^{\ast}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}[\epsilon_{IJK}\delta[n^{I}]e^{J}\wedge K^{K}]=0
.

Equation (
26
) is a manifestation of the well-known fact that the pull-back of the triad and the extrinsic curvature are conjugate variables. From the perspective of the Chern–Simons, and Ponzano–Regge quantization of three-dimensional gravity, a connection representation is more appropriate. Following
thiemann
, we consider the canonical transformation, which is generated by the Gibbons–Hawking–York boundary term

\Theta_{\Sigma}(\delta):=\Theta^{\text{EC}}_{\Sigma}(\delta)-\frac{1}{8\pi G}\delta\bigg[\int_{\Sigma}s\,\varepsilon_{IJ}e^{I}\wedge K^{J}\bigg].

(27)

Going back to (
23
), we now immediately have

\Theta_{\Sigma}(\delta)=-\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{\Sigma}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{I}\wedge\delta\Big[A^{JK}-2\sqrt{|\Lambda|}\,n^{[J}e^{K]}\Big]\equiv-\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{\Sigma}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{I}\wedge\delta[\Omega^{JK}],

(28)

where we introduced a new connection
\Omega^{IJ}
. Notice that the variation
\delta[\Omega^{IJ}]
contains a variation
\delta[n^{I}]\neq 0
. Since, however, the boundary conditions (
24
) must be satisfied, we obtain
\delta[n^{I}]\perp n^{I}
. In addition, the vector
n^{a}=n^{I}\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
lies orthogonal to the boundary, hence
{\delta[n^{I}]}\mathchoice{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{e^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}_{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}
lies tangential to the boundary. Therefore,
\frac{1}{2}\varphi_{\partial\mathcal{M}}^{\ast}(\epsilon_{IJK}\delta[n^{I}]e^{J}\wedge e^{K})=0
, such that

\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\Sigma}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{I}\wedge\delta[n^{J}e^{K}]=\frac{1}{8\pi G}\int_{\Sigma}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{I}\wedge n^{J}\delta[e^{K}]=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\delta\left[\int_{\Sigma}\varepsilon_{IJK}e^{I}\wedge n^{J}e^{K}\right].

(29)

To solve the equations of motion in terms of the new variables
(\mathchoice{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}},\mathchoice{\Omega^{{{IJ}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.72687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0906pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\Omega^{{{IJ}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2. , it is then necessary to smoothly extend the internal vector
n^{I}
into the bulk. We thus write

\displaystyle\Omega^{IJ}

\displaystyle=A^{IJ}+e^{[I}p^{J]}\equiv A^{IJ}-{\cal I}^{IJ},

(30)

\displaystyle{\cal I}^{IJ}

\displaystyle\equiv p^{[I}e^{J]}={\frac{1}{2}}C^{IJ}{}_{K}e^{K},\quad C^{IJ}{}_{K}=(p^{I}\delta^{J}_{K}-p^{J}\delta^{I}_{K}),

(31)

where
p^{I}
is an internal Lorentz vector that satisfies the constraints

p_{I}p^{I}=-4\Lambda,\quad p_{I}\big|_{\partial\mathcal{M}}=2\sqrt{|\Lambda|}\,n_{I}.

(32)

The next step ahead is to write the action in terms of the new connection
\Omega^{IJ}
. Consider first the curvature,

\displaystyle R_{IJ}[A]

\displaystyle=R_{IJ}[{{\Omega}}]+\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}{\cal I}_{IJ}+{{\cal I}}_{IK}\wedge\mathchoice{{\cal I}^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{{\cal I}^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{{\cal I}^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{{\cal I}^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}

\displaystyle=R_{IJ}[{{\Omega}}]+\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}{{\cal I}}_{IJ}+\frac{1}{4}\left(p\wedge(p_{I}e_{J}-p_{J}e_{I})-p_{K}p^{K}\,e_{I}\wedge e_{J}\right),

(33)

where
p=p^{I}e_{I}
and
\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}\mathchoice{{\cal I}^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{{\cal I}^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{{\cal I}^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{{\cal I}^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}+\mathchoice{[\Omega\wedge{\cal I}]^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{[\Omega\wedge{\cal I}]^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{[\Omega\wedge{\cal I}]^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{[\Omega\wedge{\cal I}]^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}
and we used the fact that
e^{I}\wedge e_{I}=0
. With the above expression, the first term of the action (
23
) is

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{JKI}\,e^{I}\wedge R^{JK}[A]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{JKI}\,e^{I}\wedge\left(R^{JK}[{{\Omega}}]+\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}{\cal I}^{JK}+{\frac{1}{2}}\,p^{[J}\,p\wedge e^{K]}-\frac{p_{M}p^{M}}{4}e^{J}\wedge e^{K}\right)

(34)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{JKI}\,e^{I}\wedge\left(R[{{\Omega}}]^{JK}+\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}{\cal I}^{JK}-\frac{p_{M}p^{M}}{12}\,e^{J}\wedge e^{K}\right).

Going from the first line to the second line, we used the following identity

\frac{n_{M}n^{M}}{6}\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\wedge e^{K}=\frac{1}{2}n^{I}\varepsilon_{IJK}\,n\wedge e^{J}\wedge e^{K},

(35)

for all
n^{I}:n^{I}n_{I}=\in\{0,\pm 1\}
. Returning to the definition of the action (
23
), we obtain

\displaystyle S_{\text{EC}}[e,A|n]

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{8\pi G}{\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}}s\,\varepsilon_{JK}e^{J}\wedge K^{K}=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\left[\int_{\mathcal{M}}{\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{I}\wedge\bigg(R^{JK}[\Omega]+\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}{\cal I}^{JK}\bigg)}-\frac{1}{2}{\int_{\partial\mathcal{M}}}\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{I}\wedge e^{J}\,p^{K}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{M}}{\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{I}\wedge\bigg(R[\Omega]^{JK}\,+\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}{\cal I}^{JK}}+(\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}e^{[J})p^{K]}-\frac{1}{2}e^{[J}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}p^{K]}\bigg)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{M}}{\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{I}\wedge\bigg(R^{JK}[\Omega]\,+{\frac{1}{2}}\,e^{[J}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}p^{K]}\bigg)}.

(36)

Equation (
36
) suggests to introduce a new action,

S[e,\Omega,p]=\frac{1}{16\pi G}\int_{\mathcal{M}}{\varepsilon_{IJK}\,e^{I}\wedge\bigg(R[\Omega]^{JK}\,+{\frac{1}{2}}\,e^{[J}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}p^{K]}\bigg)},

(37)

where
p_{I}
satisfies the mass shell condition
p_{I}p^{I}=-4\Lambda
. To obtain the equations of motion, we vary the action for fixed boundary conditions. Given the action (
37
), the appropriate boundary conditions are

p_{I}\big|_{\partial M}=2\sqrt{|\Lambda|}n_{I},\quad\mathchoice{h^{{{[I}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{K}}}}{h^{{{[I}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{K}}}}{h^{{{[I}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{K}}}}{h^{{{[I}\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{K}}}}\mathchoice{h^{{{J]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.33472pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.38194pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.34862pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.34862pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.54951pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.24963pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{L}}}}{h^{{{J]}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.48615pt][c]{$\textstyl (38)

where
\mathchoice{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}
is the projector
\mathchoice{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{h^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}=-sn^{I}n_{J}+\delta^{I}_{J}
, and
\varphi^{\ast}_{\partial\mathcal{M}}:T^{\ast}M\rightarrow T^{\ast}(\partial\mathcal{M})
is the pull-back. The resulting equations of motion are

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}^{IJ}

\displaystyle=R^{IJ}[\Omega]+e^{[I}\wedge\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}p^{J]}=0,

(39)

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{I}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}e^{I}+\frac{1}{4}\,C_{JK}{}^{I}e^{J}\wedge e^{K}=0.

(40)

The variation with respect to
p_{I}
is redundant. Taking into account that
\delta[p_{I}p^{I}]=2p_{I}\delta[p^{I}]=0
, i.e.
p^{I}\perp\delta[p^{I}]
, we obtain that the variation of the action with respect to
p^{I}
vanishes provided

\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{IJK}\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}(e^{J}\wedge e^{K})\propto p_{I}\,d^{3}v.

(41)

Given (
40
), this condition is always satisfied since
\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{IJK}\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}(e^{J}\wedge e^{K})=-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{IJK}p\wedge e^{J}\wedge e^{K}=-p_{I}\,d^{3}v
.

The field equations (
39
,
40
) have a simple geometric meaning. They can be rearranged into a single flatness constraint for a
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{3}
matched connection. Consider the
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{2}
Lie algebra-valued one-form

\boldsymbol{A}_{a}=\frac{1}{2}\mathchoice{\Omega^{{{IJ}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.72687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0906pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\Omega^{{{IJ}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.72687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0906pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\Omega^{{{IJ}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.72687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0906pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{\Omega^{{{IJ}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[9.17293pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[5.72687pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[4.0906pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}\otimes J_{IJ}+\mathchoice{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}{e^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.33765pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.59009pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.85005pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{a}}}}\otimes P_{I},

(42)

where
J_{IJ}
are the (Lorentz) generators of
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)
and
P_{I}
are the generators of
\mathfrak{an}_{2}
. The commutation relations are

\displaystyle\big[J_{IJ},J_{I^{\prime}J^{\prime}}\big]

\displaystyle=4\,\delta^{[R}_{I}\delta^{S]}_{J}\eta_{SS^{\prime}}\delta^{[S^{\prime}}_{I^{\prime}}\delta^{R^{\prime}]}_{J^{\prime}}J_{RR^{\prime}},

(43a)

\displaystyle\big[P^{K},J_{IJ}\big]

\displaystyle=2\,\delta^{K}_{[I}P_{J]}^{\phantom{K}}+\mathchoice{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}p_{J]}^{\phantom{K}},

(43b)

\displaystyle\big[P_{I},P_{J}\big]

\displaystyle=p_{[I}P_{J]}.

(43c)

Going back to (
3
), we identify the action of the right (left) module,

P^{K}\lhd J_{IJ}=2\delta^{K}_{[I}P_{J]}^{\phantom{K}}\in\mathfrak{an}_{3},\quad P^{K}\rhd J_{IJ}=\mathchoice{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}{J^{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[6.35211pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.89958pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.7854pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{[I}}}}p_{J]}^{\phantom{K}}\in\mathfrak{so}(1,2).

(44)

To introduce the curvature of this connection, consider first the following covariant derivative, defined by its action on the basis elements of the
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{2}
algebra,

\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}J_{IJ}:=0,\quad\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}P_{I}:=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{d}p^{K}\otimes J_{KI}.

(45)

The definition (
45
) extends naturally to all
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{2}
Lie algebra-valued
p
-forms
\boldsymbol{\omega}\in\Omega^{p}(\mathcal{M}:\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{2})
via
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}(\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}+f\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2})=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1}+f\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}+\mathrm{d}f\wedge\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}
for all
f:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and
\boldsymbol{\omega}_{1},\boldsymbol{\omega}_{2}\in\Omega^{p}(\mathcal{M}:\mathfrak{so}(1,2)\bowtie\mathfrak{an}_{2})
. Notice also that the derivative is flat, i.e.
\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}^{2}=0
. Consider then a second such covariant derivative
\boldsymbol{D}
, which is defined by the deformation
\boldsymbol{D}=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}+[\boldsymbol{A},\cdot]
. Its curvature
\boldsymbol{D}^{2}=[\boldsymbol{F},\cdot]
is given by

\boldsymbol{F}[\boldsymbol{A}]=\boldsymbol{\mathrm{d}}\boldsymbol{A}+\frac{1}{2}[\boldsymbol{A}\wedge\boldsymbol{A}]=\frac{1}{2}\mathcal{F}^{IJ}\otimes J_{IJ}+\mathcal{T}^{I}\otimes P_{I}=0,

(46)

where the deformed
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)
-valued curvature
\mathcal{F}
and
\mathfrak{an}_{2}
-valued torsion
{\cal T}
are defined as (
39
,
40
). Let us also stress that there always exists a gauge such that
\mathrm{d}p=0
, such that the notion of curvature and torsion
{\cal F},\,{\cal T}
would also coincide with (
11
,
12
). If the field equations (
39
,
40
) are satisfied, this derivative is flat, i.e.
\boldsymbol{F}[\boldsymbol{A}]=0
.

The torsion two-form
T^{I}=\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}e^{I}
is now sourced by the cosmological constant, see (
40
). On the other hand, the field equations for the
\mathfrak{so}(1,2)
Lorentz part of the curvature two-form admit solutions where the spin connection
\mathchoice{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}
is flat and
p^{I}
is constant, i.e.
\mathrm{d}_{\Omega}p^{I}=0
. Hence, there is some Lorentz gauge element
\mathchoice{\Lambda^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Lambda^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Lambda^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Lambda^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}:\mathcal{M}\rightarrow SO(1,2)
such that
\mathchoice{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}{\Omega^{{{I}\mathchoice{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[5.08472pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[3.1884pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[2.27742pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}_{{\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{J}}}}=\mathchoice{\Lambda^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[4.51207pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[3.2229pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}{I}}}_{{{K}\mathchoice{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[4.08821pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}{\makebox[2.53847pt][c]{$\scriptstyle$}}{\makebox[1.81319pt][c]{$\scriptscriptstyle$}}}}}{\Lambda^{{\mathchoice{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\displaystyle$}}{\makebox[7.20976pt][c]{$\textstyle$}}
